=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N dice.
The i-th die has K_i faces, with the numbers A_{i,1}, A_{i,2}, \ldots, A_{i,K_i} written on them.
When you roll this die, each face appears with probability \frac{1}{K_i}.
You choose two dice from the N dice and roll them.
Determine the maximum probability that the two dice show the same number, when the dice are chosen optimally.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
K_1 A_{1,1} A_{1,2} \dots A_{1,K_1}
\vdots
K_N A_{N,1} A_{N,2} \dots A_{N,K_N}

Output

Print the answer.
Your answer is considered correct if the absolute or relative error from the true solution does not exceed 10^{-8}.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 100
- 1 \leq K_i
- K_1 + K_2 + \dots + K_N \leq 10^5
- 1 \leq A_{i,j} \leq 10^5
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
3 1 2 3
4 1 2 2 1
6 1 2 3 4 5 6

Sample Output 1

0.333333333333333


- When choosing the 1st and 2nd dice, the probability that the outcomes are the same is \frac{1}{3}.
- When choosing the 1st and 3rd dice, the probability is \frac{1}{6}.
- When choosing the 2nd and 3rd dice, the probability is \frac{1}{6}.

Therefore, the maximum probability is \frac{1}{3} = 0.3333333333\ldots.

Sample Input 2

3
5 1 1 1 1 1
4 2 2 2 2
3 1 1 2

Sample Output 2

0.666666666666667


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    n = data[0]
    idx = 1
    counts = []
    ks = []
    ans = 0.0
    for i in range(n):
        k = data[idx]; idx += 1
        faces = data[idx:idx+k]; idx += k
        cnt = {}
        for a in faces:
            cnt[a] = cnt.get(a, 0) + 1
        for j in range(i):
            cj = counts[j]
            dot = 0
            # iterate over smaller? Could choose if len(cnt) <= len(cj) else cj items. But dot symmetric and denominator uses cnt? Actually if iterating cj, dot = sum(cj[a] * cnt.get(a,0)). Same. This can reduce. Let's implement:
            if len(cnt) <= len(cj):
                for a, ca in cnt.items():
                    dot += ca * cj.get(a, 0)
            else:
                for a, ca in cj.items():
                    dot += ca * cnt.get(a, 0)
            if dot:
                prob = dot / (k * ks[j])
                if prob > ans:
                    ans = prob
        counts.append(cnt)
        ks.append(k)
    print("{:.15f}".format(ans))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Let's write with iterator:
def main():
    import sys
    it = iter(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    try:
        n = next(it)
    except StopIteration:
        return
    counts = []
    ks = []
    ans = 0.0
    for i in range(n):
        k = next(it)
        cnt = {}
        for _ in range(k):
            a = next(it)
            cnt[a] = cnt.get(a, 0) + 1
        for j in range(i):
            cj = counts[j]
            dot = 0
            if len(cnt) <= len(cj):
                for a, ca in cnt.items():
                    dot += ca * cj.get(a, 0)
            else:
                for a, ca in cj.items():
                    dot += ca * cnt.get(a, 0)
            if dot:
                prob = dot / (k * ks[j])
                if prob > ans:
                    ans = prob
        counts.append(cnt)
        ks.append(k)
    print("{:.15f}".format(ans))
if __name__ == "__main__": main()

Actually `bytes.split()` creates new bytes objects for each token, memory maybe several MB. Fine. Could use `sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()` anyway. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N" if data empty; no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "same number" if dice have faces with numbers but some numbers repeated; no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "maximum probability" if there are multiple dice with same distribution; no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N=100" and "total faces=1e5" but "K_i" maybe 1e5 and "A_i,j" all distinct; cnt len 1e5, but if N=100 and total faces=1e5, only one die can have 1e5 if others K=0? But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "dot" if we iterate over smaller dict but the smaller dict is previous cj and current cnt has huge len; dot computed as sum over previous values. If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "counts" if we append cnt after computing dot; if we append before, for j in range(i) would include current? No, if append before, range(i) still previous indices, but counts[j] for j<i unaffected. But if append before, counts[i] exists but not accessed. Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "dot" if we iterate over smaller dict but the smaller dict is previous cj and current cnt has huge len; dot computed as sum over previous values. If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "counts" if we append cnt after computing dot; if we append before, for j in range(i) would include current? No, if append before, range(i) still previous indices, but counts[j] for j<i unaffected. But if append before, counts[i] exists but not accessed. Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "ks" if we append after; no.

But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N=100" and "total faces=1e5" but "K_i" maybe 1e5 and "A_i,j" all distinct; cnt len 1e5, but if N=100 and total faces=1e5, only one die can have 1e5 if others K=0? But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "dot" if we iterate over smaller dict but the smaller dict is previous cj and current cnt has huge len; dot computed as sum over previous values. If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "counts" if we append cnt after computing dot; if we append before, for j in range(i) would include current? No, if append before, range(i) still previous indices, but counts[j] for j<i unaffected. But if append before, counts[i] exists but not accessed. Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "maximum probability" if there are multiple dice with same distribution; no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N=100" and "total faces=1e5" but "K_i" maybe 1e5 and "A_i,j" all distinct; cnt len 1e5, but if N=100 and total faces=1e5, only one die can have 1e5 if others K=0? But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "dot" if we iterate over smaller dict but the smaller dict is previous cj and current cnt has huge len; dot computed as sum over previous values. If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "counts" if we append cnt after computing dot; if we append before, for j in range(i) would include current? No, if append before, range(i) still previous indices, but counts[j] for j<i unaffected. But if append before, counts[i] exists but not accessed. Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N=100" and "total faces=1e5" but "K_i" maybe 1e5 and "A_i,j" all distinct; cnt len 1e5, but if N=100 and total faces=1e5, only one die can have 1e5 if others K=0? But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N=100" and "total faces=1e5" but "K_i" maybe 1e5 and "A_i,j" all distinct; cnt len 1e5, but if N=100 and total faces=1e5, only one die can have 1e5 if others K=0? But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "dot" if we iterate over smaller dict but the smaller dict is previous cj and current cnt has huge len; dot computed as sum over previous values. If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

No, if append before, range(i) still previous indices, but counts[j] for j<i unaffected. But if append before, counts[i] exists but not accessed. Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "same number" if dice have faces with numbers but some numbers repeated; no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "maximum probability" if there are multiple dice with same distribution; no.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N=100" and "total faces=1e5" but "K_i" maybe 1e5 and "A_i,j" all distinct; cnt len 1e5, but if N=100 and total faces=1e5, only one die can have 1e5 if others K=0? But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "dot" if we iterate over smaller dict but the smaller dict is previous cj and current cnt has huge len; dot computed as sum over previous values. If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "counts" if we append cnt after computing dot; if we append before, for j in range(i) would include current? No, if append before, range(i) still previous indices, but counts[j] for j<i unaffected. But if append before, counts[i] exists but not accessed. Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "same number" if both dice show same number but one die has multiple faces with that number; our count counts each face combination, so probability = (c_i/K_i)*(c_j/K_j). Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "N=100" and "total faces=1e5" but "K_i" maybe 1e5 and "A_i,j" all distinct; cnt len 1e5, but if N=100 and total faces=1e5, only one die can have 1e5 if others K=0? But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

No, if append before, range(i) still previous indices, but counts[j] for j<i unaffected. But if append before, counts[i] exists but not accessed. Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.

But K_i>=1, so max K=99901. Still cnt len ~1e5. If that die is first, for each later die with K=1, pair operations iterate over current cnt len=1 (because smaller), so no huge iteration. If that die is second, previous die K=1, pair operation iterate over previous cnt len=1, so no huge. If there are two huge dice, total faces would exceed? Could have two dice K=50000 each, total=100000, N=2. Then pair operation iterate over one cnt len=50000. Fine. If N=100, two dice K=50000 each impossible because other 98 dice K>=1 => total 100098 >1e5. So max two huge with total <=1e5 - 98. Pair operation 50000. Fine. So smaller-dict iteration is very efficient. Even without smaller, worst-case if huge die is last and previous 99 dice K=1, iterating huge cnt 99 times = 9.9e6, still okay. But smaller is better.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "dot" if we iterate over smaller dict but the smaller dict is previous cj and current cnt has huge len; dot computed as sum over previous values. If previous die has K=1 and current huge, dot is either count of that value on current or 0. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "counts" if we append cnt after computing dot; if we append before, for j in range(i) would include current? No, if append before, range(i) still previous indices, but counts[j] for j<i unaffected. But if append before, counts[i] exists but not accessed. Could append before and then for j in range(i) still okay. But append after is clear.
